I also had a bad habit of blurting things out before they even finished asking a question. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'd say I definitely have trouble keeping my attention on it, even when I'm really trying. 

Like, I can start looking at a planning document, or I'll sit down with one of my managers, and I'll be completely locked in for the first few minutes. But then... pretty soon I realize my mind went somewhere else and I missed half of what was just said. I end up having to stop them and ask them to run it back. So yeah, even if it's important and I want to stay focused, it just sort of drifts off on me.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Like I said, it hasn't threatened my marriage or my business. I haven't missed any big deadlines or lost money because of it. It's just the day-to-day stuff that gets annoying—like the meetings running long or the friction at home. But no serious conflicts or major losses, nothing that severe.\n\n[Last Question]\nI want to make sure I have this right: when you think about your attention problems and restlessness over the past year, would you say they're more of a personal frustration that doesn't really hold you back from your goals, or do they actually prevent you from accomplishing things you want to accomplish?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / chief_executive / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still keeping up overall, but it’s starting to cost me trust at home and patience from people at work when meetings, mentoring conversations, or family plans don’t go the way they should.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I'd say it's somewhere in the middle, but mostly just a personal frustration. It's not preventing me from hitting my big goals or anything. Like I've been saying, I'm still keeping up overall. But it's not totally harmless either. 

It's just... it's starting to cost me, you know? It costs me trust at home with my wife when family plans get messed up because I'm bouncing off the walls. And at work, it costs me patience from my team when meetings drag on or mentoring conversations go totally off track because I can't stay focused. So it's not stopping me from accomplishing things, but it definitely makes getting there a lot bumpier for everyone around me.

So it's not stopping me from accomplishing things, but it definitely makes getting there a lot bumpier for everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: I hear you saying these issues are manageable and not stopping you from your major goals. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, what made me come in is that, for the past year or so, I've been having this issue where the smallest thing just pulls me off what I’m doing before I can even stop it. It could be a phone buzz, somebody walking past my office, or, I don't know, even a random thought about my in-laws. It just completely derails me, and I figured I should finally talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Beyond being easily distracted, do you find yourself feeling restless or having trouble sitting still, and do you often act or speak without thinking first?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely get restless. When I’m supposed to just sit still, like... during family dinners or at my church volunteer meetings, it honestly feels like there’s a motor running inside me. I just get this strong feeling where I need to move around or at least do something with my hands. It's really hard for me to just sit there and do nothing.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these problems with attention and restlessness? Were you like this as a child, or did this start more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back, I was definitely like this as a kid, too. It's not a recent thing. I remember back in school, teachers were always having to move my seat because I talked way too much, or I'd just get up and walk around without asking. I also had a bad habit of blurting things out before they even finished asking a question. So, yeah, it's been going on for a long time.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these issues have been around since childhood. How have they affected you in different areas of your life—like your job performance, relationships, or school when you were younger? Has this caused real problems for you?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I’m still keeping up overall. I've managed to build my business and get to where I am, so it hasn't completely ruined things. But... yeah, it is causing some real problems. \n\nIt’s starting to cost me trust at home, especially when family plans just don’t go the way they should because I can't focus or sit through them. And at work... I can tell I'm losing patience from people. When we're in meetings, or if I'm trying to have mentoring conversations with my staff, and I'm just rushing through or getting restless... it frustrates them. So, I'm getting by, but it's definitely putting a strain on things with my family and my team.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these issues have affected your relationships and work. Looking back over the past 6 months or so, would you say these attention and restlessness problems are happening most days, or is it more on and off?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely happening most days. Honestly, it's been noticeably harder to manage for about a year and two months now. That's right around the time some conflict with my in-laws started taking over a lot of our family life. Since then, the restlessness and trouble focusing have just been pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned things are causing strain with family and your team at work. Setting aside the conflict with your in-laws, would you say these attention and restlessness problems are seriously getting in the way of your ability to do your job well or maintain your relationships, or are you managing pretty well despite the difficulties?\n\nPatient: I'd say I'm managing pretty well, overall. I mean, it's not completely stopping me from doing my job or anything like that. I built my company from the ground up, so I'm used to pushing through things and making it work. \n\nIt's just... it definitely causes friction. Like I mentioned, it frustrates my staff, and it strains things with my wife when I can't sit still for family stuff. So it's not ruining my business or my marriage, but it's definitely an annoyance that makes everyday things harder than they probably need to be.\n\nDoctor: When you think about what bothers you most—the distractibility and trouble focusing, or the restlessness and need to move around—which one would you say is the bigger problem for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if one is worse than the other, to be honest. I mean, they both just kind of happen together. I'm restless, so I can't focus, or I can't focus, so I get restless. It's hard to separate them out and say which one is the bigger issue. They just both kind of get in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something important—like reading an email or listening to someone explain something—do you find yourself unable to keep your attention on it, or do you usually manage to stay focused once you really try, even if you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I'd say I definitely have trouble keeping my attention on it, even when I'm really trying. \n\nLike, I can start looking at a planning document, or I'll sit down with one of my managers, and I'll be completely locked in for the first few minutes. But then... pretty soon I realize my mind went somewhere else and I missed half of what was just said. I end up having to stop them and ask them to run it back. So yeah, even if it's important and I want to stay focused, it just sort of drifts off on me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned managing your business and marriage overall, but I want to understand this better: If you think about the past year, have there been times when your difficulty focusing or restlessness has caused you to miss important deadlines, make significant mistakes at work, or come close to serious consequences—like losing a client or a major conflict with your wife?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say there's been anything catastrophic, like losing a major client or anything like that. I'm still keeping up overall. But... it's definitely starting to cost me. \n\nLike, at work, I can tell I'm losing some patience from my team when meetings run long because I got distracted, or when mentoring conversations get derailed. And at home, it's costing me trust with my wife. When family plans don't go the way they should because I can't sit still or stay present, it just chips away at things. So, no huge disasters, but it's definitely causing some real wear and tear.\n\nDoctor: When you think about how much these attention and restlessness problems are affecting your ability to function day-to-day—work, relationships, and other responsibilities—would you say they're causing mild difficulties that you can mostly handle, or are they causing significant problems that substantially interfere with how well you're doing in life?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's more on the mild side. I mean, it's frustrating, and like I said, it causes some friction with my wife and my team at work, but I wouldn't say it's substantially interfering with my life. I'm still running my business, I'm still married, I'm still getting things done. It's just... harder than it probably needs to be. I can mostly handle it, but it's just a constant annoyance that I have to work around.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these issues are mostly manageable and not substantially interfering with your life. But I want to make sure I understand: Looking back over the past year, would you say there have been times when your difficulty focusing or restlessness has caused you to miss important deadlines, lose money or opportunities, or create serious conflict that threatened your relationships or job?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, nothing like that. Like I said, it hasn't threatened my marriage or my business. I haven't missed any big deadlines or lost money because of it. It's just the day-to-day stuff that gets annoying—like the meetings running long or the friction at home. I remember back in school, teachers were always having to move my seat because I talked way too much, or I'd just get up and walk around without asking. I also had a bad habit of blurting things out before they even finished asking a question. So, yeah, it's been going on for a long time.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these issues have been around since childhood. How have they affected you in different areas of your life—like your job performance, relationships, or school when you were younger? Has this caused real problems for you?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I’m still keeping up overall. I've managed to build my business and get to where I am, so it hasn't completely ruined things. But... yeah, it is causing some real problems. \n\nIt’s starting to cost me trust at home, especially when family plans just don’t go the way they should because I can't focus or sit through them. And at work... I can tell I'm losing patience from people. When we're in meetings, or if I'm trying to have mentoring conversations with my staff, and I'm just rushing through or getting restless... it frustrates them. So, I'm getting by, but it's definitely putting a strain on things with my family and my team.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these issues have affected your relationships and work. Looking back over the past 6 months or so, would you say these attention and restlessness problems are happening most days, or is it more on and off?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely happening most days. Honestly, it's been noticeably harder to manage for about a year and two months now. That's right around the time some conflict with my in-laws started taking over a lot of our family life. Since then, the restlessness and trouble focusing have just been pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned things are causing strain with family and your team at work. Setting aside the conflict with your in-laws, would you say these attention and restlessness problems are seriously getting in the way of your ability to do your job well or maintain your relationships, or are you managing pretty well despite the difficulties?\n\nPatient: I'd say I'm managing pretty well, overall. I mean, it's not completely stopping me from doing my job or anything like that. I built my company from the ground up, so I'm used to pushing through things and making it work. \n\nIt's just... it definitely causes friction. Like I mentioned, it frustrates my staff, and it strains things with my wife when I can't sit still for family stuff. So it's not ruining my business or my marriage, but it's definitely an annoyance that makes everyday things harder than they probably need to be.\n\nDoctor: When you think about what bothers you most—the distractibility and trouble focusing, or the restlessness and need to move around—which one would you say is the bigger problem for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if one is worse than the other, to be honest. I mean, they both just kind of happen together. I'm restless, so I can't focus, or I can't focus, so I get restless. It's hard to separate them out and say which one is the bigger issue. They just both kind of get in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something important—like reading an email or listening to someone explain something—do you find yourself unable to keep your attention on it, or do you usually manage to stay focused once you really try, even if you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I'd say I definitely have trouble keeping my attention on it, even when I'm really trying. \n\nLike, I can start looking at a planning document, or I'll sit down with one of my managers, and I'll be completely locked in for the first few minutes. But then... pretty soon I realize my mind went somewhere else and I missed half of what was just said. I end up having to stop them and ask them to run it back. So yeah, even if it's important and I want to stay focused, it just sort of drifts off on me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned managing your business and marriage overall, but I want to understand this better: If you think about the past year, have there been times when your difficulty focusing or restlessness has caused you to miss important deadlines, make significant mistakes at work, or come close to serious consequences—like losing a client or a major conflict with your wife?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say there's been anything catastrophic, like losing a major client or anything like that. I'm still keeping up overall. But... it's definitely starting to cost me. \n\nLike, at work, I can tell I'm losing some patience from my team when meetings run long because I got distracted, or when mentoring conversations get derailed. And at home, it's costing me trust with my wife. When family plans don't go the way they should because I can't sit still or stay present, it just chips away at things. So, no huge disasters, but it's definitely causing some real wear and tear.\n\nDoctor: When you think about how much these attention and restlessness problems are affecting your ability to function day-to-day—work, relationships, and other responsibilities—would you say they're causing mild difficulties that you can mostly handle, or are they causing significant problems that substantially interfere with how well you're doing in life?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's more on the mild side. I mean, it's frustrating, and like I said, it causes some friction with my wife and my team at work, but I wouldn't say it's substantially interfering with my life. I'm still running my business, I'm still married, I'm still getting things done. It's just... harder than it probably needs to be. I can mostly handle it, but it's just a constant annoyance that I have to work around.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these issues are mostly manageable and not substantially interfering with your life. But I want to make sure I understand: Looking back over the past year, would you say there have been times when your difficulty focusing or restlessness has caused you to miss important deadlines, lose money or opportunities, or create serious conflict that threatened your relationships or job?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, nothing like that. Like I said, it hasn't threatened my marriage or my business. I haven't missed any big deadlines or lost money because of it. It's just the day-to-day stuff that gets annoying—like the meetings running long or the friction at home. But no serious conflicts or major losses, nothing that severe.\n\nDoctor: I want to make sure I have this right: when you think about your attention problems and restlessness over the past year, would you say they're more of a personal frustration that doesn't really hold you back from your goals, or do they actually prevent you from accomplishing things you want to accomplish?\n\nPatient: I guess I'd say it's somewhere in the middle, but mostly just a personal frustration. It's not preventing me from hitting my big goals or anything. Like I've been saying, I'm still keeping up overall. But it's not totally harmless either. \n\nIt's just... it's starting to cost me, you know? It costs me trust at home with my wife when family plans get messed up because I'm bouncing off the walls. And at work, it costs me patience from my team when meetings drag on or mentoring conversations go totally off track because I can't stay focused. So yeah, even if it's important and I want to stay focused, it just sort of drifts off on me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned managing your business and marriage overall, but I want to understand this better: If you think about the past year, have there been times when your difficulty focusing or restlessness has caused you to miss important deadlines, make significant mistakes at work, or come close to serious consequences—like losing a client or a major conflict with your wife?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say there's been anything catastrophic, like losing a major client or anything like that. I'm still keeping up overall. So, no huge disasters, but it's definitely causing some real wear and tear.\n\nDoctor: When you think about how much these attention and restlessness problems are affecting your ability to function day-to-day—work, relationships, and other responsibilities—would you say they're causing mild difficulties that you can mostly handle, or are they causing significant problems that substantially interfere with how well you're doing in life?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's more on the mild side. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's really a bit of both. Like I've been saying, I am functioning well enough to keep up overall. The business is running, and I'm getting things done. 

But yes, it is absolutely making it harder to maintain my relationships. When our family plans don't go the way they should, it costs me trust at home.
